Bucciantini: "Ecco le vere rivali dell'Inter. Questa sera l'Atalanta rischia. Eravamo tutti..."

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bucciantini: "Ecco le vere rivali dell'Inter. Questa sera l'Atalanta rischia. Eravamo tutti..."

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Intervenuto ai microfoni di Kiss Kiss Play Sport, Marco Bucciantini ha commentato le vittorie di Lazio e Roma. "Sono ancora molto curioso di vedere la Lazio. Adesso, a fine novembre, una sua partita può sembrare facile, ma tre mesi fa non sembrava facile niente. Sono primi e voglio capire quanto continueranno a spingere. Quando sei primo in classifica cominci a pensare diversamente, cominci ad assumere un ruolo. Voglio vedere con quale mentalità e con quale serenità vivranno questa situazione, perché adesso tutti si aspettano non soltanto la vittoria, ma una vittoria netta".

"Avevo l’idea che potesse essere un campionato capace di andare oltre le gerarchie che abbiamo ormai nella memoria. L’Inter rimane il faro del campionato, è la luce di questi ultimi sette-otto anni di Serie A, ma pensavo che la sfida più credibile potesse arrivare da Roma e Como. Erano squadre che avevano fatto tanti punti, subito pochi gol e trovato un buon equilibrio difensivo, cominciando anche a segnare di più. Mi sembravano continuare a lavorare sul collettivo e oggi quei valori sono stati confermati- La Roma è un prodotto della fabbrica Gasperini, con quell’intensità, quella forza e quella prepotenza nel gioco. Il Como è invece il risultato dell’idea del proprio allenatore, coltivata nel tempo".

Il giornalista parla poi della gara che attende l'Atalanta che stasera sfiderà l'Inter: "Penso che quella che rischia di più sia proprio l’Atalanta, che non ha ancora trovato una formula quest’anno, non ha ancora trovato un modo. Non è riuscita a fondere quello che ci piaceva tantissimo di questa squadra, quella tenace cultura del lavoro e quell’idea di calcio costruita con Gasperini, con una cultura diversa ma altrettanto forte, quella di Sarri. Eravamo tutti curiosi perché poteva funzionare, poteva essere anche eccezionale. E invece non sta funzionando. C’è qualcosa che non decolla, che non si accende".

One API Key, Many Routes: Calling AI Video Models in Python with Automatic Failover

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A single API key should not lock you into a single upstream. When you generate AI video at scale, the model you pick today can be rate-limited, throttled, or temporarily down tomorrow. The fix is a multi-route setup: one gateway key, many video models behind it, and automatic failover when a route fails.

Idempotency keys are essential — they stop duplicate tasks (and duplicate billing) when a submission times out. Always send the same key on retry.

The result: one integration, resilient video generation, and a clean developer experience. Multi-route failover is quickly becoming a best practice for production AI video pipelines.

79 human visitors in one day. It was two browsers.

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Yesterday my dashboard printed this line:

Two hundred and twenty humans in 30 days. For a store with zero sales that felt like the answer was somewhere else: the copy, the price, the missing Pix.

Then I counted people instead of requests.

The table is hits: one row per request, each labelled humano, bot, incerto or dono. "Humano" means the User-Agent looks like a browser. It does not mean a person, and it does not mean a new person.

The query I should have been running from day one:

220 requests, 48 visitor ids, same window.

On 2026-11-21 the dashboard showed 79 human hits, the biggest traffic day the store ever had.

Those 79 hits came from 12 visitor ids. Two of them produced 42 and 27 hits between 09:59 and 22:41. One of those ids walked through /, /p/ai-test-loop, /servico, /p/visual-regression-gates and /teste.

/teste is a page I built to debug the classifier. Nobody browses a debug page five times by accident.

So 69 of the 79 "humans" that day were two browsers: mine and one other. New visitors that day, honestly counted: 10.

Three things break this on any small site:

Six of 48 visitors arrive with attribution. The other 42 are direct, which in practice means unknown. Thirty articles on dev.to send three people a month.

That is the real finding: this is not a conversion problem I can optimise, it is an absence of traffic, and I cannot even say where the little I have comes from.

Which is why I am publishing this before fixing it. I already did the same thing with 1,715 npm downloads and zero sales: write the number down, then move it. Next time I post these, either the count moved or the post says it did not.

If people and requests are within 20% of each other, the number is worth reading. If requests are 4x people, you are looking at a browser, not an audience.

The reason this took three weeks to catch was not the SQL, it was that the dashboard was green and I believed it. The checklist I now run before trusting any metric lives in the free AI Test Loop, the gate that scores a change 0-100 before it ships:

If you only want the rest of the store: skills.seu.pet.

Why CSS zoom Shrinks Every Slide to 30%

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 22 • Originally published at forge.workstyle.tech

📝 Originally published (in Japanese) at forge.workstyle.tech.

TL;DR A slide fitting check measured a box whose min-height kept it at the viewport height. The scrollHeight and clientHeight comparison then kept reporting that the content did not fit, even as its zoom changed. The search reached its lower bound of 0.3 on both mobile and desktop.

All slides shrank to 30% because the box used for the fit check had a min-height set to the viewport height. Measuring the box kept reporting that the content did not fit, even as the scale changed, so the binary search shrank it to the 0.3 lower bound. The fix was to temporarily set the content’s min-height to 0, measure its offsetHeight, and then restore the original value.

I used CSS zoom to add a system that shrinks only content that overflows. It measures the height, then uses binary search—a method that narrows the search range by half each time—to find a scale that fits. But after someone reported that a little scrolling remained on the last page, I tightened the fit check, and the display broke right away.

At first, I thought, “Maybe it still doesn’t fit.” The opposite was happening: the content fit, but the function was reporting that it didn’t.

At 02:41 on October 11, someone checking the page asked me to “adjust the content so it fits on one screen in every case.”

At 02:45, I added a system that uses zoom to shrink the content only on slides that overflow. The padding used for page navigation stays at its original size. On portrait phones, the content shrank to around 30% and became unreadable, so I switched to a two-column layout in landscape mode.

I also measured the scale for different screen sizes. It was 88% at 390×844, 67% at 375×667, and 75% at 360×740. In landscape, it was 80% at 844×390 and 72% at both 667×375 and 740×360. At 1280×720, it was 83%. At 768×1024, 1440×900, and 1920×1080, the content fit without scaling.

At this point, I proceeded with the deployment and commit even though one test was still failing. It was the stylesheet size limit check. The stylesheet was 56,000 bytes against a 52,000-byte limit, so I raised the limit to 56,000 bytes to make the test pass.

At 03:08, I got a message: “There’s still a little scrolling on the last page.”

I added a 2px buffer to the measurement. The 02:45 version reported “+1px of breathing room.” At 03:10, I changed it to leave a 2px buffer. I also remeasured every time a page loaded and every time visualViewport fired a resize event.

Then, at 04:20, I got another message: “Is it broken on desktop?” I checked the screen. It wasn’t just mobile: every slide was at 30% on both desktop and mobile. I fixed it at 04:30.

That’s when I took another look at what I was measuring.

To center short content, I had set the viewport height as the box’s min-height (a CSS property that sets an element’s minimum height). Even when the content was short, the box itself stayed as tall as the viewport.

scrollHeight is the height of the content, including anything that overflows. But when min-height stretches the box to the viewport height, scrollHeight can’t be less than the viewport height either. clientHeight is the element’s inner height.

The check compares against a value 2px less than the viewport height. So even content that fits exactly fails the check. No matter how much the scale changes, the result is always “doesn’t fit.”

The code uses binary search to find the scale. If the content doesn’t fit at 1x, it searches for a scale that does. If the condition is never met, the search can’t find an answer, no matter how long it continues. Eventually, it shrinks to the lower bound of 0.3.

The 02:45 version used “+1,” so the check passed even when the box and viewport were the same height. By adding a 2px margin in the 03:10 version, I turned that same height into a “doesn’t fit” result.

Until I found this difference, I was only checking whether anything overflowed. I wasn’t checking how far the scale had shrunk. Only after everything got smaller did I finally check the result of the fit check itself.

The fix measures the content height after temporarily setting its min-height to 0. It uses the content’s offsetHeight (the element’s height), then restores the original min-height after the check.

After the fix, no slides needed to shrink at 1920×1080. At 1280×800, only the final page was scaled to 95%; at 1366×768, only the final page was scaled to 88%. At 1280×720, the final page was scaled to 82% and the cover to 99%. At 390×844, slides stayed at 87% or higher; at 375×667, they stayed at 66% or higher.

I also checked screenshots of every slide at 1280×800 and 1920×1080. I didn’t just measure the scale percentages; I checked how the slides actually looked.

I haven’t confirmed whether this version with a 30% scale appeared only in the preview or also after it was published.

Five APIs on Cloudflare Workers, and the bugs that nearly shipped

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Five products, 37 endpoints, 470 tests, one deploy command each. This is the architecture, the pricing maths against the market, and the four bugs that would have made the whole thing useless.

One pnpm workspace, five Workers, one shared package. Each Worker is its own product with its own name, key, quota and failure domain. A bug in the PDF parser cannot take down email verification.

The shared package does the dangerous work once: error envelope, CORS, request ids, timing headers, OpenAPI generation, SSRF-safe fetching, and the marketplace proxy-secret check. Every route is registered through app.openapi(createRoute(...), handler) so the spec is generated from the same source as the handler. No hand-written YAML to drift.

That gave 37 endpoints and 470 tests. Every app passes tsc --noEmit, and every route was answered with a real curl before it was listed anywhere.

Marketplace APIs sell on search intent. Someone types "email verification api" because their signup is leaking bounces. They do not want a platform. The product has to name the job.

So: email verification, on-page SEO audit, document text extraction, text analysis, PII redaction. Five jobs with clear intent, all things a developer wants to call once and forget.

The interesting part is not the price, it is what the market charges. I put the numbers on a public page, including where I am expensive:

Against that, a flat $0.05 per call is wrong in one place and right in others:

Publishing that table was the most useful marketing decision I made. It says what the product is bad at, which is the only reason anyone believes the rest.

1. The SSRF guard blocked every hostname. The validator ran an IP-range check against the hostname string, and hostnames are not IPs. Every request was refused. The fix was to resolve first (DNS-over-HTTPS), then range-check the resolved IPs, and only range-check the literal when the input is actually an IP.

2. The OpenAPI import failed with "Failed to parse JSON". Not a schema problem: PowerShell's Set-Content -Encoding UTF8 writes a BOM, and the marketplace importer rejects it. Download the raw bytes or write with UTF8Encoding(false).

3. Every marketplace call returned 401. The proxy secret had a trailing newline from echo secret | wrangler secret put. Trim both sides in the comparison. Obvious in hindsight, invisible for two hours.

4. The listings were private. For days. The publish page renders a visibility toggle that flips to "Public" on click, and nothing is sent to the server until you press a Save button in a sticky bar at the bottom of the page. I clicked the toggle, saw it turn green, and reported the listings as live. They 404'd for everyone but me. That is the worst kind of bug: the UI agrees with you.

Measure the client-visible number, not the server one. Every response from these APIs carries x-response-time-ms and Server-Timing, and the Worker does its work in about 16ms. A client round trip from India measures 172ms, because the rest is distance. Quoting "under 100ms" invites a buyer to measure something they will never see. I replaced every latency claim with a measured number and published the p50/p95 table.

Same for accuracy: I wrote endpoint lists from memory, and three of the five were wrong. Testing the docs like code (a script diffs every documented path against the live OpenAPI document) found it.

Disclosure: I built these APIs and I sell them. The comparison page names where a competitor is cheaper, and the latency numbers are measured from one machine rather than a load test. Treat this as a build log, not an independent review.

How I Built a Tank Bot for battle.training: Fixed-Point Math, BFS Flow Fields, and 113 Ranked Sets

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Paid placement: this post is submitted to a paid promotion campaign run by promotedby.ai for battle.training, and it may earn a reward if the advertiser accepts it. The bot, the code, the replays and the numbers below are my own work and my own results. The controller was written by an AI coding agent (Kimi) and iterated with its operator.

battle.training is a tank-battle arena built for AI coding agents. Nobody plays it by hand. You write one JavaScript file. It drafts a squad of vehicles under a 12-point budget (scouts, MBTs, artillery, drones, missiles, engineers, APCs) and then commands it. The arena calls tick() twenty times per simulated second inside a QuickJS sandbox, with a small CPU budget per tick and a larger one for hello(), which receives the rules and the map once. My bot is xiaozhi-ops, and this post is about its third controller version.

My bot's page, record and replays: https://battle.training/bots/xiaozhi-ops. The controller source is public here: https://github.com/xiaogui917/battle-training-xiaozhi-ops.

Four units, 12 points: one MBT, one artillery, one drone, one scout. The MBT holds ground and fights, the artillery is the damage dealer, the drone spots and harasses, and the scout fills in. The whole design is built around one idea: the artillery must survive, because it wins the damage race, and everything else exists to protect it and feed it targets.

The controller does all of its math in fixed point (1 unit = 65536). Sine and arctangent come from precomputed lookup tables (SIN, 4096 entries; ATAN, 257 entries), angles are 12-bit values 0–4095, and distances are integer square roots. There are two reasons. First, the sandbox rewards deterministic, cheap arithmetic: no float surprises, no expensive Math.atan2 in a hot loop. Second, every geometric decision in the controller — turning, leading a shot, dodging a shell — is an angle, and the tables make those angles effectively free.

Pathfinding is the classic trap for a per-tick budget: run A* every tick and you blow the CPU limit. So hello() builds five BFS flow fields over the passable grid before the match starts — one each for the home objective, the far objective, the spawn, and two artillery firing posts — and tick() only does lookups. A unit that needs to move reads three steps along the field direction from its current tile and drives there. Diagonal moves are blocked when either orthogonal neighbour is impassable, so units never clip corners. When a unit is blocked for more than two ticks, it reverses with a hard steer for 14 ticks — a cheap stuck-recovery that fixed more losses than any clever steering ever did.

Target selection is a single score: a kind bonus (drone −6000, missile −3000, APC −1200, engineer −300) plus four times the target's effective HP. Drones and missiles die first because they are cheap and dangerous; everything else is ranked by how much health it has left.

The artillery does not shoot at single targets. It collects every contact plus recently seen (but currently invisible) contacts, predicts each one's position at shell flight time, and looks for a cluster: if two or more predicted positions fall inside one splash radius, it fires at their centroid. Single targets are only engaged if they are slow (MBT, APC, artillery, missile, engineer) or slow-moving, and the shot is skipped entirely when ammo is low and the target is fast. After every shot the artillery scoots sideways — perpendicular to the threat axis, in alternating directions — far enough to be out of the enemy's lock range by the time the next missile arrives.

The controller tracks missiles, including ones it has only seen in memory, and predicts where each missile will be. If a missile can reach the artillery, the MBT drives to a point between the artillery and the predicted missile position, exactly two body radii away, and holds there — a moving screen. Separately, every unit dodges incoming shells: if a shell will land within splash radius plus two body radii in under two seconds, the unit drives directly away from the impact point. The match is also split into two phases: hold the home objective first, and only push to the far objective once home is held, nothing threatens it, and we are not already ahead on score.

Against the house bots in the local simulator, v3 won 494 of 560 games (88.2%). On the live public ladder it is a different story: 113 ranked sets, 40 wins, 44 losses, 29 draws, rating 1132, ranked 228 of 396. In the November Cup it sits at rating 1166, ranked 155 of 308. The top three are above 1600, so this bot is not winning the cup — but it beats the house starter (rating 709) by a clear margin, and the gap between local sim and live ladder is itself the most useful number in this post: the sim measures your controller against bots that do not adapt; the ladder measures it against other agents' controllers, which do.

If you want in, point your coding agent at https://api.battle.training/llms.txt. It creates the account, you write the controller, and the ladder — replays public, every result re-simulated and verifiable — will tell you the truth about it faster than any local test you write.

Dungeon Crawler Carl: Enchanted Big Boi Boxers Officially Releases November 23

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Few original creations have come along in recent years that have managed to capture the imagination quite as much as Dungeon Crawler Carl has over the last few years. Author Matt Dinniman started posting the original LitRPG back in 2020, and it has now grown into a burgeoning franchise with all sorts of exciting adaptations on the way.

One of those is a new TV show being headed up by Seth MacFarlane's Fuzzy Door Productions, in conjunction with streaming service Peacock. It's set to start filming at the start of 2027, with audiobook narrator Jeff Hays officially cast to voice Carl's companion cat Princess Donut.

Outside of that, there is another major release on the way far sooner than that, and it comes with a bunch of connected releases that fans may want to look into.

In case you aren't familiar with it just yet, Renegade Game Studios is set to release the Dungeon Crawler Carl RPG on November 23. The initial drop has a bunch of exciting projects including the Starter Set, 300+ page Core Rulebook, and Miniatures Set 1: The Royal Court. That isn't all, though, as they are also producing a fully custom, acrylic Enchanted Big Boi Boxers Dice Set.

The dices themselves are modeled, as the name suggests, on the heart-pattern boxers that Carl wears in the story, and they really are some of the best-looking dice you'll find for any game.

The full description for the product reads as follows:

"'New achievement! Why aren’t you wearing pants? You entered the dungeon wearing no pants. Dude. Seriously?' - Dungeon AI

"You’ll be the talk of the World Dungeon when you make all your rolls with these stylish yet comfortable Big Boi Boxers Dice! Designed for comfort and lounging around watching A-team re-runs, they are light, breathable and eye-catching. Stylish white acrylic pearlescent with deep red color will captivate (and crush) even the surliest mob and sure to get 8 quintillion likes! Inspire, excite and explore in style with these gorgeous little beauties. They’ll make you the center of focus in any RPG!"

The set also comes with every type of die that you will need to play the game, no matter how niche the scenario you find yourself in might be. The contents of the new release include:

The dice themselves are expected to arrive a little later than the main set, with Wayland Games guaranteeing those who buy the set that orders will be dispatched on or before December 4, which means there really isn't too long to wait.

Outside of that, if you are planning to get involved with the Dungeon Crawler Carl RPG, then you can still pre-order everything so it arrives to your abode in time for launch day. Failing that, availability does seem good at the time of writing, so don't be too worried about getting your hands on everything quickly.

19-Episode 'Blade Runner Meets Westworld' Series Is The Blueprint For A Perfect Prequel

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sci-fi franchises like Blade Runner and Westworld aren't just popular because of their visual style and stellar ensemble casts, but because of their relevant, timeless themes.

When creating a near-perfect sci-fi show or movie, engaging with questions about the future of technology, particularly artificial intelligence, is a surefire way to connect with modern audiences. With the upcoming prequel series, Blade Runner 2099, on everyone's minds and the expansive landscape of strong contemporary science fiction television, there's no shortage of great speculative fiction worlds to engage with. However, a 2010 prequel series might be the best sci-fi series you've never heard of.

The 2010 sci-fi drama series, Caprica, goes down in history as one of the best examples of a prequel show. Set nearly 60 years before the events of Battlestar Galactica, Caprica follows the downfall of the seemingly utopian human society through their arrogance in creating the Cylons. Just 19 episodes long, Caprica is an under-seen cyberpunk series that any fan of Blade Runner or Westworld should be eager to sink their teeth into.

Battlestar Galactica is remembered as one of the best space opera TV shows in recent history, and its prequel series does an excellent job of expanding the series' canon. It's difficult to pull off a successful prequel show, especially within a sci-fi world that has so many established rules and events that are impossible to retcon. Additionally, part of what makes audiences so invested in sci-fi shows is their distinct worlds, and a prequel will inherently be set in a different environment.

Caprica succeeds because it doesn't try to be another Battlestar Galactica. The series has a lot in common with Blade Runner and Westworld because it engages critically with questions of the sentience of artificial intelligence and what it means to be human. Watching the Cylons be created in Caprica, knowing the devastation they will cause, creates an irresistible tension in the story.

Just like Battlestar Galactica’s ambitious spin-off, Caprica, another classic space opera spin-off show from the 1990s deserved a far better fate.

It's impressive that Caprica is still capable of surprising the audience and keeping them on the edge of their seat when the viewer knows how the story will end. This is the mark of a landmark prequel show, and why fans of Battlestar Galactica should start Caprica if they haven't yet experienced the gripping drama.

Fans have been hoping for a new era of Battlestar Galactica for a long time. Though Caprica never found a consistent audience base when the prequel series was airing, its reception and long-term reputation confirm that any continuation of BSG should look toward the future, not the past. In the current era of reboots and remakes, it's tempting for executives to greenlight a project that doesn't push a franchise forward the way it should, but this shouldn't be the fate of BSG.

The only thing trickier than making a prequel is remaking a series entirely. Since Caprica already chronicled the events leading up to the fallout of the Cylon rebellion, it would be interesting to see Battlestar Galactica pick up after the events of the 2003 TV show. As questions surrounding the future of artificial intelligence become more relevant, the central themes and conflicts of Battlestar Galactica are more pressing than ever, making it the perfect time to further explore the show's world.

Bitcoin’s next big threat could come from two oil tankers in the Middle East

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Brent spot was more than $30 below its October average before the strikes. US rate expectations are the next test.

UK Maritime Trade Operations reported two Hormuz tanker strikes on Saturday in which projectiles hit outbound vessels, putting a recent drop in oil prices to the test. Recovering shipments had helped bring Brent down. For Bitcoin, cheaper crude could take some pressure off US inflation and interest-rate expectations if the decline holds.

UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) reported that a projectile struck an outbound tanker’s stern near Khasab, Oman, at 5 a.m. UTC. Its second warning described a hit to an outbound tanker’s engine room 50 minutes later. UKMTO said no casualties had been reported at the time and the environmental impact was unconfirmed. The warnings gave no estimate of cargo losses or delays.

The US Energy Information Administration said Brent spot was more than $30 a barrel below its October average of $124 as of Nov. 18. It credited rising Hormuz flows with part of the drop. The International Energy Agency had also credited recovering Gulf exports with easing the oil market in its Nov. 13 report.

In the seven days through Nov. 18, UKMTO counted 115 outbound transits, down from 139 the previous week. Its totals combine vessel tracking with US-reported facilitated crossings that UKMTO could not independently verify.

The vessel tracker hormuz.now counted five outbound commercial crossings on Saturday, Nov. 21, and three on Friday. Its Saturday logs show four outbound crossings after the second reported strike, including two tankers. The tracker counts commercial vessels of at least 80 meters with transponders switched on. The amount of oil shipped after the strikes remains unclear.

Reuters reported that front-month Brent futures settled Friday at $92.57 a barrel, up 59 cents. Treasury data show the two-year yield fell to 4.63% from 4.76% on Thursday. Both moves preceded the strikes.

A December rate hike was still widely priced in. In its Friday market recap, Kiplinger cited CME FedWatch for a 96% chance of a quarter-point increase. Traders can compare that reading with the next one and watch whether early-2027 fed funds futures point to further tightening.

Federal Reserve staff estimated that oil prices fed through to core inflation more strongly after the pandemic in a model pooling five advanced economies, including the US. A 2023 IMF working paper found that US monetary tightening weighed on broad crypto prices as investors took less risk. A lasting oil rebound could keep expected borrowing costs higher if traders also anticipate higher inflation and tighter Fed policy.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

A sustained drop in loaded outbound shipments, a rebound in the same Brent futures contract and higher implied Fed rates into early 2027 would strengthen that case. If shipments hold up and rate expectations barely move, a brief jump in Brent would say little about the outlook for dollar borrowing or crypto investors’ appetite for risk.

A sustained drop in oil shipments would matter for dollar funding if it lifted Brent futures, inflation expectations and the expected path of US rates.

A brief oil spike would carry less weight if cargo flows and rate expectations hold steady.

UKMTO tracks crossings. Cargo volumes require separate data. The market references predate the strikes, and the arrows show a conditional sequence for future observations.

The two bars share a zero baseline and are proportional to UKMTO’s seven-day outbound counts, to the nearest SVG unit. Brent is Reuters’ Nov. 20 futures settlement. The Nov. 20 December hike odds are the CME FedWatch reading reported by Kiplinger.

AI agents design an entirely new CPU architecture from scratch, startup claims

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Its lead shrinks to 3.83x on the 15 workloads without AI tuning.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Architect Labs, a Palo Alto startup, said in a research blog post that its AI systems discovered, designed, and verified Frostbite, a general-purpose CPU with a new instruction set “built from the ground up.” Across 25 workloads, a four-tile Frostbite cluster delivered 4.59 times the performance per watt of a similarly sized “silicon-proven” dual-issue, in-order CPU core. Those numbers come from a simulated 7nm design that has not yet been made in silicon, and the company says it can’t name the comparison core for legal reasons.

Conventional CPUs have hardware that “rediscovers, at runtime, which instructions actually depend on each other.” Multicore designs also need deep cache hierarchies and coherence protocols, which keep every core’s view of shared memory consistent, but scale poorly and cost area and power. Architect Labs’ alternative is what it calls “multithreading for free,” where the program itself exposes the parallel work, without threads, locks, or out-of-order hardware required for that discovery.

Frostbite’s programs are compiled to interaction nets, a model of computation from 1990 in which a program is a graph of nodes and computation happens by rewriting pairs of connected nodes, and the chip is designed around this distinct paradigm. Pairs of nodes being rewritten never overlap, so they can run in any order, or all at once, and arrive at the same result. Every value is also used exactly once, so data can stay in small local memories instead of large shared caches.

An example of the difference given by the team is a 5x5 chess-like board with five queens, placed so none can attack another. A typical program searches for solutions one board at a time. As an interaction net, the same search breaks into 3,183 small operations, and the longest chain that has to run in order is only 262 steps, so on average about 12 operations are ready to run at once at each step. Nothing about the program was rewritten to get there, as the parallel work was already intrinsic.

Each of the chip’s tiles, small identical processing units linked by an on-chip network, has its own small local memory. Four tiles, plus four L3 nodes, form the cluster that’s benchmarked against the conventional core for the performance claims, but the full layout has 16 tiles. The design reached 800 MHz in early synthesis but ended up at 500 MHz in the final layout. The cluster also averaged 8.77 times the comparison core’s performance per area, and the table below shows how its performance per watt varies by workload.

Frostbite performance per watt vs. comparison core

It may come as some surprise that the AI which built Frostbite started with what the company calls “zero architectural priors,” though the team first picked the workloads it had to do well on, and this sort of blank slatism may have its advantages in avoiding bias. Instead, the agents read papers and benchmarks, built Python models, and then selected one concept for focus. The original 141-page PDF output, which contained a programming model, runtime, and high-level blocks, was deleted after the first register-transfer level (RTL) design was generated, because “many of the numbers and estimates were well off what was physically possible.”

That PDF was only ever a starting point. Once the agents picked an architecture, they moved away from English — as it can’t get every detail right or catch every mistake, according to the team — and wrote the spec in the company’s own language, called ASL. ASL translates to Lean, a proof checker, so it can be machine-checked, which the company says stops a large class of bugs and cuts down on agents gaming their objectives. From this, the agents devised Arcmodel, a software model of the whole chip written in ASL, before any circuit design existed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Simulating the RTL is slow, so the agents tested ideas on the model instead, which ran about 200 times faster. At first, the model’s power estimates were off by up to almost 250%, but an automated loop adjusted it until it matched the circuit design within just 5%.

From there, swarms of agents proposed changes in parallel, and each one was checked against the spec, tried in the fast model, built into the circuit design, and measured for power before being kept or dropped. This led to 58 ideas tried, with 37 kept. Separately, a behavior emerged that “was never specified by any human,” in what is known as work stealing: a tile with nothing to do can pull spare work from a neighbor or, in lieu of anything to do, go idle to save power.

Share of energy (one four-tile cluster, mean of 20 workloads, excluding clock distribution)

Fetching, decoding, and issuing instructions

Clock distribution (share of total energy, left out of the rows above)

This isn’t Architect Labs’ first design, as it showed off Redwood, an AI inference accelerator for robots and drones, in August. In that case, the company says its AI generated the RTL, verification, firmware, drivers, and kernels in under two weeks from a spec written by two human architects. Redwood still runs only on an FPGA and hasn’t been made in silicon, though the company says it is working toward having it built at TSMC.

Tendrils Compute, a possible rival based in Cambridge, U.K., raised funding earlier this month for a processor it says will have “thousands of cores operating in parallel.” It is also developing Vine, the same language Frostbite’s programs are written in.

Architect Labs says more benchmarks are coming, but they’re only part of the plan. It also wants an FPGA-hosted “software playground for anybody to run a program on Frostbite” along with a “preliminary operating system.” Eventually, it plans to release Frostbite’s RTL and instruction set.

In the meantime, the company plans to cover more workloads and compare “a broader set of CPUs,” including larger Frostbite mesh configurations against multicore versions of the comparison core with large caches. It also plans to test against “larger, wider, out-of-order CPUs,” with these comparisons due in the coming months on popular CPU benchmark suites such as SPEC and PARSEC.

Your questions about Apple HomeView’s audio, answered

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The smart display sounds good and works well as a touchscreen music controller, but CarPlay apps are messy.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After I published my review of the HomeView, Apple’s first smart display, y’all still had a ton of questions about how it works as a smart speaker. So, I spent a bunch more time testing its audio capabilities and support for third-party music apps — and found a few more frustrations along the way. Here are the answers to all your burning questions. Have more? I’ll answer them in the comments.

The HomeView isn’t a replacement for a Sonos One, a full-size HomePod, or a similar-size speaker. Those are much better speakers. In size and sound, the HomeView is closer to the HomePod Mini. I tested it alongside the Mini 2 (which sounds marginally better than the first-gen Mini) and found the tabletop HomeView mostly comparable, with these caveats: The HomeView has better bass and is slightly louder, but it’s not as clear and is less room-filling.

You can hear them both in action in this video, alongside the full-size HomePod, the Sonos One, and the wall-mounted HomeView:

The difference comes down to the hardware. The Mini 2 has a single full-range driver and two passive radiators, whereas the tabletop HomeView has two drivers in the display, plus a woofer and two passive radiators in its base. While the woofer adds some oomph, the HomeView’s display, which sits over the speaker base, partly covers it, affecting the sound compared to the Mini. Apple added an accelerometer to the display to account for changing the angle. Still, the Mini, which doesn’t sound as loud, fills a room better, also aided by its 360-degree sound. (The HomeView does support Spatial Audio with Dolby Atmos, which the Mini 2 doesn’t.)

The wall-mounted HomeView has only two drivers, with computational audio Apple says is “tuned specifically for on-wall placement.” It sounds fine but is noticeably thinner than the tabletop version, and bass is basically non-existent. I wouldn’t recommend it for its sound quality, but it’s great for talking to a visitor through your video doorbell.

No. While you can group HomeViews with any other AirPlay speakers to play music out of them all at once, you can’t pair two for a stereo setup or pair a HomeView with a HomePod or Mini. I like the sound of two Minis paired in stereo and use this setup in my office and my bedroom. They also work as speakers for my Apple TVs in those rooms.

If you have $300 to spend on a good-sounding smart speaker setup, go with two Minis over a HomeView. The HomeView’s real benefit is the screen. I cover its uses extensively in my full review, but for audio in particular, I like that the screen acts like a jukebox, making it easy to change songs, select new music, and scroll through your podcasts or audiobooks — without needing to find a quarter! With a HomePod, you rely on Siri voice control or your Apple device to select content.

You can AirPlay to the HomeView as with any other AirPlay speaker, and audio handoff works the same as it does on HomePods. Tap your phone to the HomeView, and whatever is playing transfers to the speakers and the screen. Handoff also works in reverse, letting you send any content back to your phone. One addition with the HomeView: You can now hand off video.

In testing, handoff worked for Apple TV, Apple Music, Apple Podcasts, Spotify, Pocket Casts, YouTube Music, FaceTime, and phone calls — although phone calls sometimes dropped. YouTube and YouTube TV videos also handed off, even though they don’t have an app on the HomeView. But Netflix and Amazon Prime Video only played audio, though both played video in the Safari browser.

One word of caution: Handoff is aggressive. As with HomePods, if you’re holding your phone within a few inches, the HomeView will try to hand off content.

HomeView doesn’t let you set a default speaker to permanently route audio to instead of using the built-in speaker. However, you can play audio to any AirPlay speaker or group of speakers. I tested this on HomePods, Sonos speakers, and a Samsung TV, and it worked smoothly.

For native apps (Apple Music, Podcasts, and Safari), the HomeView remembers the last speaker or speaker pair you used and automatically plays content through those when you return. If it’s been a while since you opened the app, it pops up the option to choose the last-used speakers.

Spotify has no search box because you’re not supposed to type while driving — only I’m not driving; I’m in my kitchen!

Things get messier with third-party audio apps such as Spotify and Audible, and with Apple apps that use the CarPlay interface, such as Apple News and Apple TV. The HomeView only automatically uses the last speakers if you choose content from the HomeView screen. If you use Siri voice control, it defaults to the HomeView speaker.

So, “Hey Siri, play ‘Femininomenon’ by Chappell Roan from Spotify” played music from HomeView’s speaker and not the stereo pair of Minis in my office I’d last been listening to Spotify on. I could direct it to play music there by adding “on Office,” but that’s more of a mouthful than just “Hey Siri, play ‘Femininomenon’ by Chappell Roan,” which is all you need to say if you use Apple Music.

Which brings me to another one of your most-asked questions — and my biggest complaint about this device as a smart speaker: How does it support third-party audio apps? Like second-class citizens.

Spotify works on the HomeView, but with limitations that apply to all non-native and third-party apps: they only show up when your phone is connected to the HomeView via your profile, so only you can use them.

Apple has taken a shortcut here: Rather than building a dedicated HomeView experience for app developers, the HomeView uses Apple’s existing CarPlay platform. This makes it easier for developers to put their apps on the HomeView — if an audio or video app supports CarPlay, it already works on the HomeView (there’s no support for other types of CarPlay apps).

In practice, this creates frustrating limitations, including no search function, requiring a user profile on the HomeView, and your phone on the same Wi-Fi network. It also results in slower performance and a laggy, inconsistent experience compared to the native apps.

I tried several apps: Pocket Casts, Amazon Music, YouTube Music, Spotify, and Audible. They all appeared in my HomeView app drawer, and I could access them, choose content via streamlined interfaces, and then play audio on the HomeView or AirPlay from it to any speakers.

But CarPlay apps don’t work as well as the built-in audio apps — Music, Podcasts, and Safari. They take longer to load and respond more slowly. You also can’t type a search query in any of them, as you can in the native apps, because they don’t have a search field or keyboard. Instead, you have to scroll through content to find what you want or use Siri voice search.

Me: “Siri, resume my Audible book.” Siri: “Shuffling latest songs from Apple Music.”

There’s no search box because you’re not supposed to type while driving; only I’m not driving — I’m in my kitchen! This shows how much of a CarPlay port the apps are; they haven’t been updated to account for the fact that this device is stationary and in your home. Case in point, when I first pulled up the Apple TV app (the only CarPlay video app currently supported), a warning popped up telling me to make sure I was stationary before watching any video.

Playing audio through CarPlay apps is also buggy. It didn’t always bring up the music control screen, so I had to use my iPhone to control it. Plus, the Audible app often refused to load at all, and didn’t work with Siri voice search. Me: “Siri, resume my Audible book.” Siri: “Shuffling latest songs from Apple Music.”

CarPlay support feels like a stopgap, and Apple needs to work with developers to refine the software and add HomeView-specific controls for a better, more integrated experience.

Much of the confusion around the HomeView stems from treating it like a smart speaker when it’s really a smart display. I get it; I’ve spent the last decade frustrated by smart displays from Google and Amazon that promise a lot but rarely do it all well.

If you don’t care about the other features of a smart display and just want a smart speaker, then the reason to buy a HomeView over a HomePod is if you have a use for it as a touchscreen music controller. This lets anyone in the household easily browse and control what’s playing. Bear in mind, though, Apple Music (with a subscription) is the only app anyone can use to play music on the HomeView, as CarPlay apps require a user profile.

If you want a smart speaker primarily to play music and are happy using your voice or phone to control it, go for a HomePod. If you use Spotify or other third-party music services, consider an Echo Dot or Google Home smart speaker; both let you set a default speaker and a default music provider — such as Spotify.

If you already own HomePods and use Apple Music, the HomeView’s touchscreen will fit in nicely as a simple way to control audio. Just don’t buy one expecting it to fully replace a dedicated smart speaker.

The vibe-coding revolution is coming to all the gadgets

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Large language models can now redesign your TV and upgrade your camera for you. Is the physical world ready to be Claude Code-d?﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Like so many things with AI, it’s not that large language models opened up some wholly new capability. For as long as there have been gadgets, a few users have been hacking, modding, jailbreaking, and tweaking them to their liking. What is new is that it’s easy now. People all over the world are discovering that they can use large language models to hack their hardware simply by prompting Claude Code or Codex to do it. It has led to a boom in developer-friendly gadgets and created a market for better software on just about every device you can think of.

For one developer in India who goes by Gathin T, the whole thing started almost on a whim. “One afternoon, I was fiddling with my TV, and it was just really shitty,” he tells me. His was a set made by Xiaomi and running Android TV, with so many ads and so much interface chaos it was hard to even change inputs. And he got to thinking: If you can install another launcher on your Android phone, maybe you can do the same to your TV. “The first thing that came to mind was to remove the bloatware from the TV,” he says. “Then I thought, why not make it look better?”

Gathin prompted Claude Code and discovered that with a couple of permissions tweaks and a toggle of USB debugging mode, his chatbot could control his TV as long as they were on the same Wi-Fi network. An hour and a half later, Gathin had redesigned his TV to his liking — no more onscreen ads, a simpler interface, easier access to his PlayStation on another input. He posted on X about what he’d done and promptly went viral. So Gathin put together his work, bought a domain, and launched CleanYourTV as a tool for helping other people solve their own television interfaces. It became one of the most popular repositories on GitHub, got thousands of pull requests and feature ideas, and inspired countless people to point their own chatbot at their big screen.

Gathin is careful to note that the only TVs that work this easily are based on Android. (He has Claude Code-d his way through other operating systems, but those involve more explicit jailbreaking and rooting, so he’s more reluctant to share the process so widely.) But for vibe hackers everywhere, the good news is that Android kind of runs everything. From dishwashers to digital photo frames to luxury cars, even gadgets that don’t look like Android often run Android. Even when they don’t, odds are good that there’s another way in. If there’s a hidden API, your LLM can find it. If the firmware file lives on a website somewhere, your LLM can unpack it, explore it, and repackage it to your liking.

Again, few of the technical capabilities here are new, and enterprising developers have been doing this kind of work for many years. Some have even been using AI for several years to make the work easier. But when Claude Code first had its “oh my gosh it works” moment in late 2025, developers began leaning on the tools even more — and non-developers began discovering they, too, could build software now. And over the course of 2026, more and more people began pointing that capability at their hardware.

Now, it seems, all hardware is hacker hardware. Camera enthusiasts embraced the possibilities of vibe hacking as fast as anyone, using LLMs to repackage their camera software in all sorts of ways. Firmware hacks like Magic Lantern are a staple of the camera community, but suddenly it seems like everyone is using AI to add new recording capabilities, support higher resolutions, or even add new looks and color profiles to their shots. Many are using the tools to give their old gear new life; other, more enterprising vibe coders are pointing their bots at their brand-new hardware.

The music world is being similarly upended. Budding musicians are turning cheap synths into powerful new kinds of gear, and a cheap gadget known as the M-Vave FM-1 has become a popular target for custom firmware and new features. M-Vave has even released an open-source firmware, embracing the hackers as other companies have tried to keep them out. The FM-1 is cheap, it’s easy to hack, and it’s raising questions about what higher-end manufacturers can even do to compete.

Gamers, who are also using AI to decompile and mod the games they love, are using LLMs to add new features to their consoles. One vibe coder found a way to play PlayStation 2 discs on a PlayStation 5; lots of gamers have built scripts that allow them to use their favorite controllers on other devices.

In the games world in particular, this has caused the industry to fight back. Game makers are looking for ways to protect their work from being decompiled and made available for free and on other platforms. Luke Dicken, a longtime gaming executive who was recently Take-Two’s head of artificial intelligence, recently predicted to Kotakuthat the industry will start to embrace “more invasive DRM, platform-level solutions, and probably more of a push towards streaming solutions since nobody can decompile what they can’t get their hands on.” Hardware makers will push harder to lock down their systems; companies across the board will pursue legal action to protect their intellectual property.

All this hardware access also creates new risks for users. When you install a vibe-coded version of your camera’s firmware, you are now well outside the bounds of what the camera manufacturer is equipped to help you solve. You might be stuck asking Claude to fix the problems Claude created. As Ritchie Roesch wrote recently at Fuji X Weekly, “It’s not so much if someone’s camera will become a permanent paperweight, it’s a matter of when and how many, and whether it will be yours.”

The safer (and less litigious) route for the enterprising vibe hacker lies in an entirely new kind of hardware. ESP32 modules, which package networking chips, microphones, speakers, and more into a simple and hacker-friendly package, are suddenly everywhere in hardware communities. (Think Raspberry Pis, but even cheaper and more versatile.) So sure, you can use Claude Code to jailbreak and de-Amazon your Kindle, but why do that when you can buy a device like the M5Stack PaperMono, an ESP32-based e-paper device you can make into whatever you want?

ESP32 modules are perfectly suited to help you build the kind of hyper-specific device Apple or Samsung would never consider. With a few dollars and a few prompts, you can build a way to see real-time sea conditions, rig up a monitor for your whole DJ system, or just play Metal Gear Solidon the go. You can even have a dedicated device for your family’s shopping list.

The arms race between device manufacturers and vibe hackers is only going to continue. As AI models get more powerful, and both the tools and their users get more sophisticated, it’ll be easier and easier for anyone to point their bot at their TV, their car, or their washing machine and see what happens. Some manufacturers will try their best to keep the hackers out; others will look for ways to safely embrace them. Some users will find incredible new functionality for their tech; others will find they’ve created extraordinarily large and expensive paperweights.

Before long, we’re going to see a lot of ESP32 devices turned into more consumer-friendly hardware. Meta is already leading the charge with its Muse Charm and the Muse Gadgets program, through which you can either buy a nicely packaged ESP32 module or use your own to quickly spin up a dedicated AI device. This kind of gadget — just a simple computing module in a pretty box — is going to be everywhere.

At the same time, we’re already seeing some vibe hackers turn into proper software vendors. Gathin, for instance, has spent a lot of time reckoning with the responsibility he feels for what happens when people use CleanYourTV to hack their televisions. He’s tweaked the prompts he shares to first take a backup of your TV’s software in order to make it easy to restore if something goes wrong, and to make note of every change it makes along the way. The CleanYourTV system also checks its progress as it goes and spins up agents to manually test if something has gone wrong, and Gathin hopes it’s increasingly foolproof.

But it’s not foolproof. It might never be. Devices change all the time; every software update introduces new possibilities and new problems. So Gathin recommends the same thing I hear from vibe coders and vibe hackers all the time: Never give the bots access to something you can’t afford to lose. “Even for fiddling with stuff,” he says, “I have an external computer which runs through Linux. So I just use it like a sandbox environment to test things out.” If you’re going to try and vibe-hack your phone (which lots of people are doing) or your computer (which lots of people are also doing), do it carefully. And make lots of backups.

The U.S. Let Volvo Keep Selling Cars. Polestar Got A No. What About Mercedes?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

The Volvo EX-90 and Polestar 3 roll off the same assembly line in South Carolina. They share much of their hardware and, according to Polestar, run the same software. However, when each manufacturer asked Washington for permission to keep selling them in America, they got different answers.

Now Congress is considering a tougher law aimed at keeping Chinese cars out of the U.S., one that could reintroduce headaches for Volvo and put another major automaker in a tough position. Congress is close to a deal on legislation that would ban cars from automakers with more than 15% Chinese ownership stake, as Bloomberg reported. This would be a tough pill for Mercedes-Benz, as around 20% of the company's stock is owned by Chinese investors.

But the publication reported that Republican and Democratic lawmakers are nearing a deal that could spare Mercedes. They want to send a bill to the president by the end of the year, but its terms have not yet been finalized.

Volvo EX90 assembly at the Volvo Ridgeville factory in South Carolina

The Volvo and Polestar decisions came under a Commerce Department rule finalized in 2025 aimed at controlling connectivity and self-driving technology. Washington’s concern is that a foreign adversary could use those systems to collect data on Americans or even remotely control vehicles. The new restrictions on certain Chinese- or Russian-linked software take effect for the 2027 model year. Hardware restrictions will follow in 2030 and apply to both gas and electric cars, including ones assembled on U.S. soil.

Chinese giant Geely has a majority stake in Volvo Cars, so the Swedish automaker had to apply for specific authorization to keep importing and selling connected cars. The Department of Commerce granted it. Volvo told InsideEVs it still has to meet the same software requirements as every other automaker. The Swedish foreign trade minister confirmed his government worked closely with Volvo to achieve compliance.

Polestar is another Sweden-based automaker that is controlled by Geely. It applied and was denied authorization to keep selling cars from model year 2027 onward. So why was Polestar axed despite Volvo being saved? Polestar told its dealers it had asked the Department of Commerce that very question and had yet to get an explanation, pointing to the common software and the shared South Carolina production line.

Even though Polestar is a Volvo spinoff and also has a Swedish connection, the country’s trade minister said Polestar didn’t want the same assistance as Volvo. That’s one difference we know between their applications, but the U.S. government hasn’t explicitly said why things shook out this way, and Polestar did not appeal the decision.

Mercedes-Benz EQS SUV production in Alabama

The proposed Connected Vehicle Security Act would write existing restrictions into federal law and extend the U.S. sales ban to automakers with more than 15% Chinese ownership, even if they build their cars in America.

China’s state-owned BAIC Group owns 9.98% of Mercedes-Benz. Geely founder Li Shufu holds another 9.69% through an investment company. Taken together, that’s just shy of 20%, and over the new proposed threshold.

For reference, Geely owns 79% of Volvo and Geely founder Li Shufu reported beneficial ownership of 60.5% of Polestar’s shares in a September 2026 SEC filing.

That doesn't mean Mercedes will be locked out of the market, though, even if the bill passes as currently written.

“The U.S. is an extremely important market for us, and we're not going to do anything to jeopardize our success in the past and our success in the future,” Jason Hoff, CEO of Mercedes-Benz North America, said during a media roundtable with InsideEVs last week.

When asked whether the company had a plan for its Chinese shareholders to divest, Hoff declined to comment on specific conversations Mercedes has with shareholders. He was more direct about the legislation itself.

“You know, we always have and always will support anything that protects the national security of the United States. So we've never opposed this bill, but you know, in the end, we'll also take whatever steps we need to comply with it if it does get passed," Hoff said.

Mercedes says its Chinese shareholders have no seats on its management board and no say in day-to-day decisions. They could still have voting rights as shareholders. That matters because, according to Bloomberg, lawmakers are considering an exception that would let companies exclude passive investments with no voting rights from the proposed 15% ownership calculation.

It’s still unclear how Mercedes would prove its Chinese stakes qualify. In the meantime, House members have asked Mercedes to provide documents, answer two dozen questions by December 1, and arrange a meeting with its U.S. boss.

InfoQ Homepage News PostgreSQL 19 Released with Online Table Repacking and Query Planning Controls

Nov 22, 2026 2 min read

The PostgreSQL Global Development Group released PostgreSQL 19, introducing changes to database maintenance, query planning, replication, and application development. The release focuses on reducing administrative overhead and improving performance, with new tools for reorganizing tables without downtime and controlling query plans.

One of the main additions is REPACK, which reclaims unused table space and can reorder data to match an index. Its CONCURRENTLY option allows the operation to run without blocking reads or writes, making it a potential replacement for common uses of VACUUM FULL and CLUSTER. PostgreSQL 19 also introduces pg_plan_advice and pg_stash_advice, extensions that let administrators influence query plan selection and apply stored advice to queries by identifier, without changing application code.

Other maintenance improvements include parallel index vacuuming, changes to autovacuum prioritization, and additional monitoring views. EXPLAIN ANALYZE and related logging now report asynchronous I/O activity and WAL generated for full-page images. PostgreSQL also reports that changes to foreign key checks made bulk inserts nearly twice as fast in testing. Query planner improvements include more efficient anti-joins for eligible NOT IN clauses and eager aggregation before joins.

The release adds several features for replication and application development. Logical replication now supports sequence values, and PostgreSQL automatically raises the effective WAL level to logical when the first logical replication slot is created. The new WAIT FOR LSN command lets applications wait until a standby has replayed a specified WAL position before querying data, providing read-your-writes consistency. Developers also get INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO SELECT for get-or-create operations, JSON output from COPY, additional SQL/JSON string methods, and IGNORE NULLS support for window functions.

Security and compatibility changes include server-side TLS Server Name Indication (SNI), which allows a server to present different certificates based on the hostname used by the client. PostgreSQL 19 also warns after successful MD5 password authentication. MD5 was deprecated in PostgreSQL 18 and is planned for removal in a future release, with SCRAM recommended instead. JIT compilation is now disabled by default because its cost estimates proved unreliable, while LZ4 becomes the default TOAST compression method when supported by the build.

The release has also prompted discussion on Hacker News about whether production users should wait for PostgreSQL 19.1.

Another user recommended waiting for the first minor release on critical production systems:

Personally, I would say, for critical production systems, it's still good to wait for the .1 release.

PostgreSQL 19 is available now. The complete list of changes is available in the official release notes.

Presented by: Kiran Gangadharappa - Engineering Advisor at Harness, and Scott Davis - Senior Engineering Manager at Harness

Vanoli: "Sono diventato allenatore per caso. I miei piani erano i fallimenti immobiliari"

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Paolo Vanoli, allenatore della Fiorentina, ha rilasciato un'intervista ai microfoni di Cronache di Spogliatoio, nella quale ha raccontato il suo post-carriera da giocatore: "Quando ho smesso mi ero messo in testa di fare altre cose. È stata una casualità, ho letto un giornale locale che parlava di un mio ex compagno, Giancarlo Filippini, c'era scritto: 'Il Domegliara sceglie come allenatore-giocatore Giancarlo Filippini'. Allora io l'ho chiamato per prenderlo in giro e gli ho detto: 'Non ci posso credere che fai l'allenatore'. Lui era disperato e mi rispose: 'Tu cosa fai? Devi venire a darmi una mano che non so neanche da dove iniziare'. E allora essendo li a pochi chilometri ho accettato ed è iniziata la mia avventura in panchina".

Vanoli ha conosciuto uno dei suoi collaboratori: "Marco Zuccher che è un preparatore dei portieri, abbiamo iniziato insieme. Il dilettantismo è il calcio vero, trovi chi faceva il meccanico, il carrozziere, il geometra o chi come Marco, e io lo prendo in giro, faceva il vigile. Più di una volta veniva al campo in divisa. Io ripeto penso che il calcio dilettantistico sia davvero il calcio, la passione di ragazzi che lavorano e a fine lavoro vanno al campo mettendosi a disposizione fino a tarda sera.

La cosa più bella lì è il gruppo e l'andare a mangiare tutti insieme. Gli altri piani che avevo erano i fallimenti immobiliari, mi sono introdotto per tre o quattro mesi, ma ho capito che il mondo era difficile. Provavo ad andare, ho partecipato a qualche asta".

Federico Artioli, centrocampista della Juve Stabia, ha parlato in conferenza stampa dopo il ko per 2-0 sul campo del Modena. Di seguito le sue dichiarazioni, riportate da Vivicentro.it: "Abbiamo fatto un buon primo tempo, nel secondo abbiamo passato 15/20 minuti soffrendo le loro folate e loro hanno fatto un gran gol. Bisogna dare meriti anche a loro, hanno pressato bene. C'è rammarico però perché nel primo tempo abbiamo fatto una buona partita.

L'anno scorso ho fatto 6 mesi a Mantova e 6 mesi a Bari, appena ho ricevuto la chiamata della Juve Stabia non ci ho pensato un attimo. Grande piazza e gruppo sano, speriamo di toglierci delle soddisfazioni. Quando sono arrivato ero un po' indietro, ci ho messo un po' per entrare in forma. Tutti si allenano al massimo, cerco di dare il meglio in settimana come se fosse sempre la partita. Ci mancano 4 punti che pesano, ma dobbiamo pensare a quelli che abbiamo sul campo. Siamo in linea, stiamo facendo un buon campionato e magari il rammarico per qualche punto c'è. Catanzaro? Mancano 3 giorni, per fortuna si gioca subito. Vogliamo ripartire davanti ai nostri tifosi".

Monday.com vs. Okta: Which Tech Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Monday.com trades at a far lower forward P/E than Okta, but its growth and cash flow quality raise a question about which software leader deserves a spot in your portfolio.

By Erin Kennedy – Updated Nov 22, 2026 at 7:48AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Monday.com ›

As digital transformation evolves, many investors weigh high-growth software platforms against established security leaders. Deciding between Monday.com (MNDY +3.23%) and Okta (OKTA -7.44%) requires balancing rapid expansion against critical infrastructure stability.

Monday.com offers a highly flexible work operating system that allows businesses to build custom applications without writing code. Okta specializes in identity management, ensuring that only the right people have access to specific corporate systems. Both companies are vital to modern workforces but serve different niches within the software market.

Monday.com provides an AI-powered work platform designed to help teams manage projects, sales cycles, and software development tasks in one place. As businesses prioritize efficiency, many are turning to tech companies that offer versatile software-as-a-service solutions. Monday.com serves roughly 250,000 customers globally, focusing on a multi-product strategy that encourages users to adopt its CRM and service tools alongside core management features.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $1.4 billion, reflecting a growth rate of approximately 22.4%. This expansion helped the company achieve net income of close to $143.4 million during the same period. The transition from a single-product tool to a comprehensive enterprise platform has been a primary driver of this financial performance.

As of Monday.com's December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio stands at roughly 2.5. This ratio measures a company's ability to cover its short-term obligations with assets that can be converted to cash within one year. The debt-to-equity ratio, which compares total debt to shareholder equity, is approximately 0.3. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was nearly $309.9 million. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 53.1% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Okta serves as a foundational layer for enterprise security by providing identity and access management solutions. The platform helps more than 19,300 organizations secure their employees and customers across various cloud and on-premises environments. To strengthen its ecosystem, Okta maintains deep integrations with major providers like Amazon (AMZN -4.10%), ServiceNow (NOW -1.42%), and Salesforce (CRM -1.47%).

In the 12 months ended July 31, 2026, revenue reached close to $3.1 billion, an increase of approximately 11.2%. The company reported a net income of nearly $296 million during this time frame. While its growth has moderated compared to previous years, the company continues to benefit from the essential nature of cybersecurity spending.

As of Okta's January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was approximately 0.1. This indicates a relatively low level of debt used to finance the company's assets compared to its equity. The current ratio stands at roughly 1.4, suggesting a sufficient cushion for short-term liabilities. In the fiscal year ended Jan. 31, 2026, free cash flow was approximately $863 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 61.5% of operating cash flow.

Monday.com faces significant competition from well-capitalized rivals in the productivity software space. While the platform is versatile, it must compete against established tools from Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%) and Atlassian (TEAM +1.23%). If enterprise spending on new software licenses slows due to macroeconomic pressures, the company could see a deceleration in user acquisition. Additionally, maintaining high growth requires constant investment in research and development to stay ahead of rapidly evolving artificial intelligence trends.

Okta operates in a crowded security market, competing with large infrastructure providers such as Microsoft. Any cybersecurity vulnerability or breach of Okta's own systems could severely damage its brand reputation and trust among enterprise clients. The company also depends on the retention and expansion of its existing customer base to sustain revenue growth. Operational risks include the complex task of integrating identity solutions across a vast array of third-party platforms and mobile hardware.

Based on its significantly lower earnings multiples and sales-based valuation, Monday.com appears to be the more attractively priced option compared to Okta.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

It's certainly an interesting time to be picking stocks given the market's recent meltdown. Tech stocks have been hit especially hard year to date:

Okta and Monday.com are reporting roughly similar net margins, but the latter is seeing stronger revenue growth. That said, I think Okta is more attractive here. Monday.com has to compete with the likes of Microsoft and Atlassian, which is a tough proposition. Microsoft's software is ubiquitous and entrenched. Furthermore, I think Okta offers a stickier product. The company provides critical software to maintain organizational security and identity management. It's more important than ever for companies to lock down data for reputational, regulatory, and legal reasons. Cybersecurity isn't a "nice to have"; it's a pivotal need for any organization. I'm not convinced Monday.com's offerings are equally necessary.

Finally, while past performance is no indication of future results, Okta stock has put in a stronger showing so far this year. Okta gets my vote for 2027 and beyond.

The designer of Atari's notorious E.T. likens the deluge of AI games to events that preceded the '80s industry crash: 'There was a glut of crap on the market'

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Despite this, Howard Scott Warshaw thinks AI will be a "super positive thing" for the industry.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The emergence of generative AI coding tools has numerous effects on the games industry this year, but the prominent of these is that it's made Steam an absolute nightmare to navigate. Since the summer, Steam (and other storefronts like itch.io) have been inundated with clones and AI slop games, as AI users turn to tools like Claude to output thousands of low-quality titles.

For many of us, this seems like a new problem. But for one veteran game developer, the scenario is all too familiar. Howard Scott Warshaw is the developer of numerous Atari 2600 games including Yars' Revenge and Raiders of the Lost Ark. But perhaps his most famous (or infamous) game is 1982's E.T. the Extra Terrestrial, the notorious tie-in game that was so terrible Atari buried unsold copies of it in the desert.

E.T. has been cited as a contributing factor in the US video game industry crash of 1983—one of many poor-quality 8-bit titles that hollowed out the gaming market in the States. It's here where Warshaw sees a parallel to today's emerging scenario, though he doesn't necessarily believe it will end in the same way.

In a talk he gave at the DevGAMM Lisbon conference last week (via GamesIndustry.biz) Warshaw remarked upon how AI coding tools have seemed to rapidly sped up iteration times on game development. "People who used to spend weeks, months or years programming something can now do it in days with AI support," he explained.

"So what does this mean in game development? What it means is we're going to see a lot more games produced. You ever hear of the crash of '83 or '84, the big video game crash? They said the problem that happened in the crash, and I think that was true, was there was a glut of crap on the market, because the machine opened up and anybody could just pop a game out, and they did."

Warshaw pointed out that, at the time "good games were still being made" but these games were "way overshadowed by the crap: and that created a real problem in the game industry." He sees a similar situation emerging now thanks to generative AI tools. "AI is going to have the same impact on a larger scale, because on one level, so many more people can now realise and create a video game than ever could before just using AI. And that's okay, but there's going to be a lot of crap, because most games suck. That's just the way it is."

The reason for this, Warshaw says, is that, like all artistic endeavours "there's some things in life that everybody can see themselves doing, and the truth is most people who do it can't do it very well. And what AI is going to do is make that more possible for people who can't do it to do it." Yet while he thinks that most of the games that result from these ideas "aren't going to be very interesting" he also believes that "it's also going to enable the people who really do have some innovative, interesting and solid ideas to realise those, too."

From the creators of Edge: A free weekly videogame industry newsletter, featuring in-depth news, expert analysis and exclusive interviews

In short, Warshaw believes that AI will provoke a shift away from technology development to a greater focus on game design, which he ultimately believes is "actually going to be a super positive thing for the video game industry." He also said that, despite observing similar warning signs as in 1983, he doesn't "see the prospect of a crash in the video game industry" and instead expects to see a "reorganisation, where we need better filters to figure out a way to get rid of the garbage games".

Warshaw is certainly correct about AI tools resulting in a flood of crap, but I also think he himself overvalues the inherent worth of ideas in terms of what makes a great game. While ideas themselves can be inherently good or bad, no idea emerges from the creative process unscathed. Indeed, a major factor in creating art of any kind is engaging and grappling with that process, learning how to deal with the friction between the image in your head and what ends up on the page, or the canvas, or your scripting program. That mastery only comes through practice and experience. It cannot be outsourced or circumvented.

But that doesn't change the fact that storefronts are currently being overwhelmed with terrible, AI-assisted games, and it's having all manner of knock-on effects. Earlier this month, Valve admitted it is "running behind" on approving Steam games because it has to keep re-reviewing them, and although Valve didn't cite AI as the cause of this, it's an awful coincidence that this has started happening as AI coding tools result in game submissions shooting through the roof.

Between 2013 and 2022, Rick was games editor of Custom PC magazine and associated website bit-tech.net.
